ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e5795	Mrs. Federal Minister! Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! Austria is first and foremost in terms of quality of life. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Since the reform government has been ruling this country together with this National Council, Austria's position in the success statistics of the 194 countries of the world has improved from 24th to 13th place. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Head of State Wallnöfer once said: Austria is a "rock-rich" country. We have a lot of stones. – He has expressed that we cannot, like other countries, base our wealth on raw materials – steel, ore, oil – but our "red material" are the people who have been well-trained in this country, who have made this country the seventh-richest country in the world. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] If we are discussing university reform today, we are discussing this "raw substance" about how we continue to enable people in this country to train themselves to the best, and how we are providing industry with the research results it needs to maintain and develop the economic and work location of Austria at the high level that we all want. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Education and training, science and teaching, research – this not only affects the around 200,000 students in this country, but not only the approximately 20,000 university staff, the professors, assistants and general university staff, that is very important for them all, but it also affects the country as a whole. Science, education, teaching and research bring this country forward, and therefore university reform is so important. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Ladies and gentlemen! The new Federal Government has therefore written a comprehensive educational reform in its programme. Mrs Gehrer has already taken two important steps together with us: We have introduced the study contributions. This was first very, very criticised. Today we know that this was an important step forward. It has also, together with us, introduced a new right of service for university staff – with the agreement of the social partners, the public service union. That too was an important step. Today, Mrs Gehrer submits the university reform to this House, which not only gives us an important discussion and decision-making material, but also makes herself a very beautiful birthday present. We want to congratulate her – she has a round birthday today! [[It is a pleasure to see the ÖVP and the liberals, as well as to the SPÖ representatives.]] Ladies and gentlemen! We all know that university reform is absolutely necessary. The need for reform is undisputed. For several years now, it has been known – and the respective government has known – that: We need high schools, we have to go to universities in the 21st century. ==References== We have to take them out of the dead end where she led the Firnberg/Kreissky University Reform of 1975; a dead end leading to the demise of universities, a dead end that led to the constipation of universities that slowed young scientists, a dead end that led to the fact that universities could not develop sufficient management, a dead end that led us to fall back in the international competition of universities. [[Abg. You son of a bitch, go, stop!]] We want to change that. We want universities to move forward and be among the best in this world! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] It's the third university reform I'm experiencing: 1964 the University Organization Act of Heinrich Dremmel, it brought the legalisation of the universities; 1975 the higher education reform of Hertha Firnberg and Bruno Kreisky: it brought the strengthening of universities in commissionitis, in legalization, in predominance of meeting university customs, in the repression of university freedom – and yet the universities have performed great achievements; despite this reform and not because of this reform. This is the third great reform that I am experiencing, and I hope that what Minister Tuppy has begun with his reform, that where the third funds... – Mr Schwemlein, you will show white ignorance if you do not know that Tuppy at the time has made a university reform that has brought third-party funds to universities, without which it would not exist the research we have today. [[Abg. Sweam: Just the Tuppy!]] [[Ironic hustle and bustle at the SPÖ]] [[Intervocations at the ÖVP]] [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Erhard Busek, who had already developed universities in the right direction in 1993, took another step towards reform, but at that time we were inhibited by the social democratic coalition partner. Now we can unabated a reform according to the conceptions of the 21st century. ==References== Mrs Bundesminister, we are grateful for your reform proposals. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Full self-government takes place in the place of state flaws. In place of annual budget struggles and beggaring at the Ministry of Minorities for special donations, full financial autonomy enters a budget that is secured for three years. Instead of these state acts, there is a contractual partnership, just how the partnership is a fundamental idea of this government. In schools we want the school partnership, at universities we want to co-determination, self-determination and partnership agreements between the high schools and the Republic. We shall therefore put this principle in place of the state of affairs. And to replace the rigidity in a fixed concept of old-age service law, the more flexible law of employment, which also brings to universities something that they have not had so far, namely, the work constitution, according to the Works Constitution Act works councils, so that the other staff can participate to the extent that it is possible in all Austrian businesses. This is an important progress also for the workers and employees at the universities. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Ladies and gentlemen! The university's freedom of association replaces previous concepts of dependence. We will set up universities that finally have a powerful management, so that the many management errors that characterise today's universities are abolished: Space shortage, too small lecture rooms for students, too few exam dates, too few exercises. The Austrian high schooling has rightly shown a whole series of shortcomings in the processes at the universities. We have put money into universities, but so far we do not have the structures that allow students to exercise their rights – to take exams, to attend lectures and to train themselves. With the university reform, the university itself will bear the responsibility for this. We will continue to give research and teaching to the university's self-management. In other words, management will be the responsibility of the rector and the rectorate; for all matters of science, the Senate will be there with the participation of students, university professors, middle-level construction and staff; and as a control body, the University Council, borne by the trust of the majority of the university, will replace the previous state influence and exercise control. Ladies and gentlemen! This is a new structure that will free up the Austrian universities, which have already done great things. It is a new structure that will lead universities to new top results; a new structure that will provide a real opportunity for those who work at the university and those who are trainees for the future. ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e6023	Mr President. High house! Ladies and gentlemen! Yesterday, the new university law could be presented to the Council of Ministers in 2002; the new university law, which represents a milestone in the history of universities, the new university law that we have discussed and discussed in more than 300 events, discussions, discussions and discussions for over a year. I would like to thank all those who have been constructively involved in this discussion. Thank you. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] In the course of this discussion, however, I have also experienced the constant spread of many fears, many prejudices. That is why I would like to start by stone and say clearly what is not the case. It is not the case that universities must earn a third of their own budget. They will receive the entire budget from the state. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] It is not true that the extraordinary professors have no rights and no obligations. You have all rights and all obligations. They are very important and very necessary, we need them at the universities. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] It is not true that there will be no more organization at the university. Of course, each university will be divided into faculties, institutes, departments. But it is very important that this organization can be made by the university itself. It is also not right that there will be no more participation. We have provided for the establishment of collegial bodies, and for the first time we have determined that students have even more participation in study matters. At least 25 percent of students must be represented on all matters of study. It is also not true that the University Council will be the political influence for the ministry. More than half of the university council is elected by the university itself. It was very important to me to clarify this at the beginning. I am also very glad that the students now know that these are untruths that are being scattered. Of the 194 776 students we have, only 60 still believe such untruths. These 60 were with me yesterday in the ministry, and I hope we managed to explain the situation to these 60 too. [[Abg. Dr. Jarolim: This is an untruth!]] [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] It is very important to me that we see in this university law the common objectives we want to implement. The common goal is that universities, instead of laws, regulations and decrees, can now live autonomously under modern target agreements that they can live a independence that has been stated as the goal of universities for many years. I also thank those who have thought with us – especially the university members, including the rectors, who have contributed far too much to good developments. I have always said that well-founded proposals are taken very seriously by us. In the context of this reform, we have also made some further developments on the original draft. So I'm saying, The universities receive independence with a secure budget. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] I am very grateful that, in good negotiations with the Minister of Finance, we were able to achieve that there is no solid budget. A cover has been laid on the budget for all previous allocations. – The museums are currently working, for four years, with a budget covered. The universities receive their basic budget, a realistic budget, and the budget is dynamic, because the universities receive the increases in salary, the increased social benefits, the costs of changing to a new accounting system and the rents they have to pay to the BIG. It is therefore a budget where the additional costs will be reimbursed and the basic budget will be fully maintained. – My warm thanks goes to the Ministry of Finance, the Minister of Finance, who was a good negotiating partner for us. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Ladies and gentlemen! It is also important to me to note that we have rethinked the student participation – based on a mandatory third party, the commissions and commissions, which the students have often said themselves that it is not so interesting to be in these many commissions. For the first time, the governing principles of a university law – I quote – have been adhered to: "Consultation of students, especially in matters of study, in ensuring the quality of teaching and the use of the study contributions". That's what young people want too. They want to be able to talk about what the study contributions are used for. They want to be able to speak with them on the subject of study, as well as with the curriculum and quality offered to them. There is a mandatory evaluation with publication and with consequences. This must be stated in the service agreements, in the statutes. The student participation was thus rethinked. In the law of study, which is also contained in this law, students also had the possibility that four examinations would be available to them and that they would have fixed three examination dates per semester. That was a big concern for the students, and that was accepted. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] We have designed a new, efficient administration for universities: an administration with a university council as a strategic body, with a Senate as an academic body and a rectorate as an operational body. It is a slim administration that brings together responsibility and decision-making power. There is therefore a new management. I would also like to clarify the following: Of course, the rector – or hopefully a rector – is given competences to the other organizational units. Of course, he will pass on the sovereignty of the staff through the institutions, the budgetary responsibility for the institution budget as in the case of a procura and, of course, the responsibility will also be transferred. But the overall responsibility for the university is finally established with these three institutions. It is very important to me that there are clear structures, clear responsibilities and clear responsibilities. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Ladies and gentlemen! One thing also concerns me: There was so much talk about the so-called middle building, about the extraordinary university professors and professors. There are more than 2 800 at the universities, and they are a very important part of our universities. They are important for teaching, research and support for students. They were reclassified in our law as "other university teachers". And the transitional provisions stated that their tasks, their rights, but also their duties remain natural: in teaching, in research, in student care, in taking examinations and in managing diplomas. All that they have done so far remains fully preserved. In the course of the discussion, it was also found that there are many extraordinary university professors who are very committed, and that, if the professors propose them, they should also have the opportunity to become head of the institute. Thus, their important tasks at the universities were retained. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Ladies and gentlemen! I'll get you together. The University Law 2002 brings independence with secure funding, it brings together decision-making and responsibility, and it brings internationalisation. There is a better basis for universities to be able to compete in international competition. We can therefore say with some pride: We are on our way to the world class with this university law 2002!

The universities wanted to be excluded from the camera list. – They get them through this reform. The universities wanted a partnership principle with the public. – They will receive it and will be released from the administration and from the sovereignty principle. ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e7710	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! High house! Today, when we discuss this educational popular desire, which was carried out in Austria almost a three-quarters ago, we are discussing more than 200 000 signatures by Austrians, who have expressed their concern and dissatisfaction with developments in the Austrian education system in petitions and citizens' initiatives. Ladies and gentlemen! No one of us can or should today deny that changes in our society, changes in our culture and in our economy make completely new demands on our education system – not only since the discussion on education-population. I think I can see that more than ever, good education and training will decide whether a young person can meet the challenges of tomorrow and whether he has grown up to them. Now we need to note that we don't know the world tomorrow in at least detail. But what we expect is certain outlines, and some corner or fixed points can certainly be called: Internationalisation will be a challenge, a variety of cultures will all surround us, and I also believe that there will be an enormous increase in knowledge and information, as well as new technologies that we already have in the midst of us. Furthermore – and this is a special challenge – profound changes are to be expected in the individual work organisations. Ladies and gentlemen! If we want to continue to participate in the knowledge society, we need to define in time: What is a sustainable education system? What should it look like? . I think it is high time to enter a public debate on a sustainable education system. In a few words: For us Social Democrats, a sustainable education system must be a broad, flexible offer of high quality – a wide range that ensures and allows access to all pupils, all young people, and ultimately all adults – in order to involve lifelong learning as well. [[Congratulation with the SPÖ]] The education system – now I join the past debate – must be democratic and solid. It must be dynamic, flexible and permeable, and it must be based mainly on the abilities, requirements and possibilities of the students. The school equipment, the maximum number of students, the group sizes, the allocation of resources, the teacher service law, the performance assessment and many other things, as well – all of these are self-evident things that I do not want to address extra. The most important and probably top goal of school education – which is now the level below – will and must be that students develop gradually, but consistently to motivated, interested and largely self-learning people. Only then will we have the opportunity to enable more people than before to learn life-long learning or to take it seriously. Ladies and gentlemen! This also significantly alters or widens the target dimension of education. Education offers personal orientation in an increasingly complex world – a first important point. Education enables the participation and design of personal and social life, and finally education is the key to the labour market and the basis for economic development. I therefore say "sufferer" because there has been a wealth of suggestions from the experts, and because we rarely have the opportunity to sit together with such many highly competent experts and to discuss these important things. I would have expected that we would at least have had a discussion in the context of the general debate. For example, if I recall the proposals and considerations of Professor Schilcher, Professor Rathmayr, Professor Lenz or Dr. Strasser, I can say that there were options, there were proposals that were suitable to take the opportunity to have a comprehensive discussion. In education, in the citizens' initiatives and petitions, only the key points and pressure points of the day were addressed and taken up. Let me now comment briefly on this in telegram style. A massively criticised and therefore included in the educational population’s desire was the invading introduction of the study fees, which resulted in the fact that there was a drop in students by almost 20 per cent in 2001/2002 alone, that there was an alarming drop in female study students and that there was a decrease in the number of students in the study year 2001/2002. In the meantime, 82 percent of students already have to be partially or fully employed, so that they can still afford to study at all. I now criticise the fact that the concept of a working student does not exist at all until now and also in the new university draft. There are no specific offers for this group. There are no appropriate time adjustments, there are no structural adjustments either. This leads to the fact that these same students have much longer study periods and, of course, although they hardly consume them, they have to pay each semester for their study contributions. If you say that there is no need for action here, ladies and gentlemen, then I cannot understand! The budget-accompanied laws and the financial compensation – you know – have introduced savings measures in the field of education. They deny these savings measures. However, if you look at the reality of the classes, the schools, you need to take note of – we have a lot of contacts – there are a number of deletions of additional education courses in class: For example, special funding lessons and measures for the integration of children with non-German mother tongues are reduced, afternoon care is called into question, as are free goods, non-binding exercises and some more. But there is no need for action on all of this! The problem still unsolved in the field of secondary education was a very important point of discussion. You know the problem. We have a fight in the agglomerations for schools in the AHS sub-level, we also have the driering primary school. We already have 50 and more percent of students in the AHS sub-level in cities with more than 10,000 inhabitants. In the rural regions, the AHS sub-level is not accessible, and the primary school is the school for all. Then we discussed school forms of cooperation. Experts and MEPs shared the view that cooperation is necessary, and they are already taking place in many places – cooperation across all types of schools: Primary school – primary school, primary school – AHS, secondary school I, secondary school II, educational academies, universities and much more. We have also found that cooperations are particularly important at the seas, i.e. folk school – secondary schools, primary school – secondary schools and so on. It's all good and right. But one thing you could or did not want to allow, namely the establishment of cooperations, of open, flexible cooperations in the field of secondary education. We wanted a uniform federal arrangement for new forms of cooperation, but unfortunately there is a no, because you see no need for action either. Ladies and gentlemen! The constantly rising number of school students also leads to a more difficult situation in our schools. Today we have 350 classes in Austria, in which 36 and more students sit. They always try to calm us down with averages, but the real situation in these 350 classes can only be disastrous – because we know that high schooling numbers make a modern education almost impossible, because we know that high schooling numbers make individual care more difficult in primary schools, and because we also know that school-centred teaching in these classes is no longer possible. Directly related to this problem is the insufficient number of schools, especially in middle and higher schools. We have heard from experts that in the current school year there have been 7 000 rejections to secondary and higher schools. Hofrat Skal has told us that the situation in vocational schools is extremely problematic, that teaching has become extremely difficult and that this is the cause of the high dropout rates in upper secondary schools. [[Abg. Amon: This has not been said by Scale!]] We have proposed more school room, more schools, more value units, more teachers, more use of free and empty classroom, because we know that in fashion schools, in agricultural schools, in primary schools... – Mr. Schweitzer! [[Abg. May. Schweitzer: How do you pay that? How do you pay that? How do you pay that? – Make a suggestion!]] Here's a technical discussion in progress, your calls don't help me now. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] Our offers have not been heard because you see no need for action. [[Abg. May. Schweitzer: Do you know what I want? For two people a teacher!]] [[Call the SPÖ: Take-off hunters!]] [[Abg. May. Schweitzer: what you want to pay with the interception hunters!]] I come to the conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, but before my conclusion I mention the unbearable situation in the apprenticeship market. They certainly know that in March 2002 8 000 apprentices had no teaching place, so they did not have the opportunity to attend the vocational school, and that this is the cause of many young people having to quit their school career in advance. They know that we have around 7 000 pupils per year of age, who no longer make further schooling after compulsory school. It has been clearly established that there is a very strong socio-economic breakdown in Austria. That is why PISA's call is clear: Austria and Germany are countries where the average economic, social and cultural status of schools has a significant impact on student performance. The reduction of socio-economic segregation between schools is a possible strategy to address this problem. So it's relatively simple: PISA recommends starting from this early separation in primary schools and gymnasia and the subsequent social effects. There is certainly no link between class size and learning; this is evidenced by studies, for example the most recent at the University of Koblenz-Landau on mathematical achievements at the eighth level or by the University of Marburg on cognitive performance, and the first evaluations of current school statistics in Austria confirm this. I personally am not surprised by these results. I know big and small classes from my own education. In my opinion, efficient class management or the quality of the class has always depended mainly on those who teach there and not on whether a class was larger or smaller. In some cases, the maximum number of students, especially in the lower classes, was far above today's standard, yet there were excellent degrees and a high level of education and large group membership. ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e11630	We now come to points 2 to 6 of the agenda on which the debate is being held under one. ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e11663	Thank you, Mrs. Minister! Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I know it is a further way from the Austrian education system to French Guiana or to Cotonou in Benin, that city after which the Cotonou Agreement we are talking about today is named, but I still hope to be able to gain and claim your attention to these issues. We are discussing several agenda items in this debate. Let me first briefly explain why my group will not approve the report by the Committee on Foreign Affairs on the declaration by European governments on the production phase of the Ariane carriers; we will be very pleased with the other proposals. There are three main reasons for this disapproval. One is that, although in the agreement with the Ariane carriers there is very much inside that these missiles may be used exclusively for peaceful purposes, the risk of abuse is of course always given. A second point is that this space centre is located in French Guiana, is being tested there and, in my opinion, it is very likely that colonialist aspects do not necessarily justify approval. The third and probably most important argument is that even in the context of the agenda issue we are discussing here today, namely the Cotonou Agreement, the financial resources can be used much more effectively than for the research of an Ariane rocket and the use of it, especially in view of the fact that the issue of poverty reduction is a main issue of the Cotonou Agreement – and also of Austrian development cooperation. That is why I think it makes sense for our group not to give its approval to this agreement. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] We shall, however, give our approval to the other proposals and also to the Cotonou Agreement. Let me explain why. This new agreement between the European Union and the so-called ACP States, the African, Caribbean and Pacific States – 77 of which have signed 76 of this Agreement; Cuba is not in the process, but it is now trying to do so; I hope that this will succeed – it regulates an area that has been the subject of a whole generation of agreements since decolonization and since many African, Caribbean and Pacific states became independent. This is therefore a broad, above all developmental, but also economic and political cooperation agreement. It replaces the Lomé IV Agreement and the various other agreements that have been concluded between this group of countries and the EU since the 1970s. Originally, these agreements had a very partnership spirit. It was really a matter of taking action together to ensure these countries a wider and better social and economic development. Unfortunately, the Lomé IV Agreement, which entered into force in 1990, had already gotten a little away from this partnership idea. At the time, integration into the world market was a priority and the objectives of the independent development of these countries in the social field, in the environmental field, were put into the background. However, this principle of solidarity with the African, Caribbean and Pacific states – which represent former colonies for some European countries – is probably one that should still be a thing of the past. For example, in the context of the Lomé Treaties, there were individual points that were innovative for the time being – a time when, as is unfortunately the case today, very uneven world market conditions and exchange conditions for products from these countries were established. There were the famous STABEX and SYSMIN agreements. They provided for the granting of compensatory payments to ACP countries for agricultural products and for mining products which could achieve very low prices on the world market, in order to support production in the countries and thereby increase the revenue of these countries and their population. That was a really innovative element at the time. These two measures are no longer being continued, and there is no real substitute for what I am going to explain later on, which would compensate for the failure caused by the conditions of the world market. A positive point in the Lomé IV agreement has already been that human rights were enshrined as a fundamental element of cooperation and measures could be put in place, so that, if a country did not meet these criteria, this would also mean an end to payments. This happened, for example, in 1991 with Sudan, where a military coup took place in 1989. At present negotiations are taking place again. Especially those two colleagues, who were with me in Sudan at the beginning of April, know that negotiations are now under way and that the EU is very much pressured to reclaim the criteria in the area of human rights, democracy and the acceptance of civil society. At the end of the 1990s, efforts were being made to reform these agreements. Lomé IV ran out in 2000, and it was clear that there had to be a reform at the roots, as Commissioner João de Deus Pinheiro put it at the time, which meant a reform to the head and members. He expressed in the Commission's 1997 Green Paper that he said economic developments in individual countries went hand in hand with the resolution of growing social structures, with increasing military conflicts and humanitarian disasters. He also said that these economic developments, one-sided in the world market, also led to development efforts being undermined because the focus of EU assistance in the area of disaster aid and crisis management had to be shifted and funds could no longer really be invested in social and economic development. There was therefore a need for reorientation, and these new negotiations were led by the motto that there must also be a new opportunity in the new millennium to re-establish the EU's relations with the ACP countries. In this sense, this agreement is very much a necessary and important regulatory instrument with which the EU's relations with these 76 countries – two thirds of the developing countries – can be reformed. In reality, however, this agreement remains far behind the promises made by the EU towards these countries. I believe that there are many shortcomings in this paper, some of which I would like to mention. For example, as I have already mentioned, the idea of partnership is no longer present, but all economic relations are to be subject to the rules of free world trade, especially WTO rules. At the same time, this agreement states that the regional partnership should be improved. It's totally unclear how one should fit in with the other. In fact, there should be a priority for regional cooperation and then, so to speak, a trade fair on the free market. Another point is that although the agreement contains very positive wording in relation to the political dimension, it mentions the promotion of participation in the countries themselves. For example, the question of human rights I mentioned earlier is further defined, but the participation and participation of civil society organisations, women's organisations and environmental organisations is also defined on paper. As the implementation looks, there is a fear that the motto applies: Paper is patient. What the implementation looks like is not yet contained in this paper. It will show how the negotiations between the EU and individual country groups that will begin in September 2002 will look like the so-called Economic Partnership Agreements, the European Union's concrete negotiating mandate. There is already a proposal from the Commission, which is likely to be adopted by the Council in June. I am very curious about this, and I would have liked to ask the Foreign Minister – I do not know whether Mrs Gehrer can answer this, but I hope that I will have the opportunity to address this specific question to the Foreign Minister herself – whether she intends to make a number of points in this negotiating mandate to the European Union: the regional integration priority that has already been mentioned in relation to integration into the world market; the question of whether environmental sustainability and social sustainability should also be a priority in implementation, and to point out that it would be necessary for EU countries and ACP States to jointly try to reform the WTO rules in this regard, and not just to say: We are now focusing on the WTO in everything. I'm sorry, but the In this context, however, it is particularly important to point out a point that my colleague Pirklhuber will then carry out even more precisely later: EU rules in the field of agriculture are probably those most relevant to developing countries. Subsidies and export promotion are a major disadvantage for developing countries in particular when it comes to placing their products on the market or being able to implement their products only in their own country. We see this lack of coherence also in Austria, but unfortunately it is also evident in this paper: On paper – paper is patient – the objectives are, the aim of combating poverty is also well formulated in words, but what it will look like will depend on the EU's negotiating mandate. It will still be necessary for the Austrian side to ask for the two points already mentioned, but also the point of civil society's participation in the negotiations for these new EU Economic Partnership Agreements with the ACP countries, and I insist that the Austrian Foreign Minister will also do so, even if she cannot answer them now.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e12512	Mrs. Federal Minister! Mr President. High house! The Council of EU Ministers for Development presented or decided in November 2000 the EU's new development guidelines, which are intended to continue to be the priority objective of the EU's development policy to combat poverty. In addition, the international division of tasks should focus on trade, regional integration, transport, macroeconomic reforms, particularly in the field of education and health, food security, rural development and the promotion of democratic structures and the rule of law. In addition, EU development ministers have also recognised the importance of spending 0.7% of EU countries' gross domestic product on development aid and cooperation. With the Cotonou Agreement now in force, which was signed on 23 March, the Council adopted a Decision on the conclusion of an Agreement between the European Economic Community and the Republic of Moldova on the conclusion of the Agreement between the European Economic Community and the European Economic Community on the one part, and the Republic of Moldova on the other hand, on the other hand, on the conclusion of an Agreement between the European Economic Community and the Republic of Macedonia on the one part, and the Republic of Macedonia on the other hand, on the other hand, on the other hand, on the The core of the Agreement is a liberalisation of trade relations with the ACP States within the framework of new regional economic partnership agreements: Within the next phase of preparation from 2000 to 2008, existing trade preferences will continue to exist, but then there will be WTO-compatible agreements to promote and ensure full trade liberalisation in the transition period from 2008 to 2020. The financing of this Partnership Agreement shall be through the European Development Fund and through loans from the European Investment Bank. What is the new thing about this agreement? – First of all, the anchoring of political dialogue at all levels: both regionally and over regionally. Secondly, you, Mrs Gehrer, have already said this: good governance; in addition to democracy, the rule of law and human rights as an important principle of cooperation. Further: Increase the efficiency of cooperation, new trade arrangements through regional free trade agreements and, above all, the increase in financial volume. What are the criticisms of this agreement? . Some of my previous speakers have already said that although the situation of this new Treaty is to be welcomed, the Lomé Partnership spirit has, to a large extent, been subject to the rules of free world trade. While the fight against poverty is still at the heart of this agreement – the priority of the EU's practical development policy, it is a key word: 0.7% of the gross domestic product, but it must only be included in this agreement, and the necessary funds should be allocated to it. It is also necessary to ask whether these economic partnership agreements, which de facto aim at full trade liberalisation, can be in line with the guidelines of ecologically sustainable development and, above all, socially sound development. The EU's agreement with the Republic of South Africa has given a small foretaste of how forced market opening can seriously damage the economy of a contracting partner. In reality, regional South African products have been almost completely suppressed by cheap EU exports, thus causing serious damage to the economy of South Africa. Although the issue of equality between women and environmental protection provisions has been addressed in the agreement, as far as real implementation is concerned, it is relatively vague, and it is also left to a large extent open as to how this whole thing should be implemented. So we basically welcome this agreement and its ratification, so much so, even in the light of the existing criticism, that cooperation between developing countries and industrialized countries can be made partnership in the future and that the benefits of globalisation can also be used for developing countries. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e12588	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! I am pleased that today the Cotonou Agreement is ratified by the Austrian Parliament and on 26 March. ==References====External links== Why? . I believe that all this may be far away for Austria and the people living here, but it will become more and more important for us to see how the development of the Third World countries will be in the future. The Cotonou Agreement also makes it clear to us one of the most pressing problems facing the more developed countries – thank God also for those who otherwise close their eyes to poverty – namely the need for developing the Third World, namely, the problem of migration, which – with a new and partnership approach – is at least addressed. The agreement addresses the issue of poverty reduction, as well as the possibility of creating preventive instruments on the basis of the Cotonou Agreement on conflict prevention in the face of internal political problems in a developing country. It is clear how important the solution to the migration problem is to be found and how to deal with it: On the one hand, EU citizens who are legally resident in the EU of the Contracting Partner States are finally being treated as EU citizens when they live and live within the Community – something that is incredibly important for the integration of these people and their stay here. At the same time, the possibility was also created or the commitment of the partner countries to reintegrate illegal persons in the EU, as well as stateless persons, into their countries. It has often been criticized that this passus is included in the agreement, so I would like to address it directly. I think it is important that the agreement at least contains a regulation on how to deal with those people who were often "interparked" in intermediate and extradition camps in the future. I also think it is important that we, as rich countries, are given the financial resources to the Third World countries in order to actually implement the reintegration and reintegration of their citizens. In addition, I would like to say quite globally: It is not a perfect agreement, but it is a further, big and important step with which you can really be satisfied. It is now also facilitated for the first time, on the basis of this agreement, to prevent cases of serious corruption. Very often, the best development policies in the countries of the Third World and especially in Africa have not been implemented and not implemented because the governments there were corrupt. Now there is at least one way to respond accordingly at an early stage and before conflict can be started, thus contributing to conflict prevention and combating poverty spirals. Ladies and gentlemen! I hope and am pleased that you will give all of this agreement your consent. I hope that this is just a step on the way in the right direction. Thank you.

At the time, we set a time limit for a new, uniform right to award until the 1. ==References== Ladies and gentlemen! We've done it, and we're all glad about it, and that's the mood now. It certainly wasn't easy. We can all be proud that in constructive negotiations we have come to a conclusion that industry and science have long called for. That is what we really did with this result. Allow me to call you a few numbers to show you what we're talking about here. These figures are impressive. The right to award is, I say, an extremely important economic law. We are talking here about a public-hand order volume, which in 1999 amounted to a total of €35.3 billion – that is, €484.7 billion schilling. This is an enormous volume, which is 17.9 percent of GDP – this is only so that we know what we are deciding on today. In 1988 it was still 15 billion euros or 207 billion schilling, i.e. more than a doubling of this volume in eleven years. As a result of this volume, I believe that it is justified – and I would like to go a little further than the accusations made by Mrs Petrovic or her – that we are here very carefully looking at what the legal situation is, what the constitutional situation is and what a law has to look like. The task of the contract law is to aim at public procurement and to ensure proper control; given the huge sum, it is an important task. On the other hand, however, manageable, simple rules should be created. That is to say, this law represents a walk between "as much as necessary" and "as little as possible". Simplification, which was implemented in this proposal for a law, was urgently needed. There has also been a harmonisation in the field of competence, and if you look at Article 14b – its own constitutional provision – you see that the legislation will be federal in the future, unless it is legal protection, and enforcement is based on whether it is an order of the federal government or of the countries. There was also the desire of the countries, in my opinion, to be involved in the legislative process in good time in the negotiations, and I believe that we have done so in a constructive and good way. Therefore – and with that I come back to Article 14b – this article 14b looks a bit complicated. But that was necessary. A simpler legal language was not possible here. The involvement of the countries is ensured by having a right of participation before and a right of consent to the procurement. I would have liked to have – and it had already been quite a long way in the negotiations – that there would have been a majority of the countries' right of consent. I'm sorry it didn't happen. Yesterday I read in a newspaper that a certain criticism of the involvement of the countries has come back. A contractor has so far faced the difficulty of being subject to different contract rules in Vienna-Liesing than in the neighbouring Perchtoldsdorf. The best bidder principle has also been given priority in the future over the low-cost bidder principle. There is only the lowest bidder principle for standardised services, where the only difference is the price. As far as fees are concerned, we have also been able to negotiate moderate fees at low order values. The new right of procurement brings speeding up the procedure, brings more transparency and, above all, brings fairness to SMEs. In the urgent request submitted today, the Greens asked the Federal Government, and in particular the Federal Minister for Agriculture, Forestry, the Environment and the Water Industry, to take their own Council of Ministers decision of 30 June. By April 2002 implementing an Austrian Sustainable Development Strategy, in particular the objective of a gradual implementation of a socially balanced environmental tax reform and tax benefits by 2005. For this proof of confidence in this government, we must be grateful to the opposition to and for itself. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] This one point, however, is only a tax in the discussion. This sustainable strategy package sets out 20 objectives. It cannot be reduced to a single goal, but it is a long-term programme. The transition to sustainable development is a challenge that requires a fundamental, comprehensive reorientation of all areas of life in politics, society and the economy. It is important to promote a development that meets the needs of today's generation without endangering the potential of future generations, for in the past future generations have been burdened. One has, as it were, built up one's own standard of living at the expense of the future generations. The EU has committed itself to reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 8 per cent by 2012 and Austria by even 13 per cent, below the 1990 level. The main measures to achieve this objective are to increase energy efficiency and increase the use of renewable energy sources. The share of renewable energy sources in total energy consumption is expected to be doubled from 6 percent to 12% in the EU by 2010. We have already completed the practical test in Austria. In Austria, the share of renewable energy is already 12 percent and could still be doubled to 24 percent by 2012. We have huge reserves, especially in biomass. There are, of course, also measures. I only mention as an example: We have a housing promotion in Salzburg, according to which there is more housing support for the square meter with renewable energy – without hydropower, but through biomass or solar panels – for every square meter by 1000 S. The result is that 74 percent of all newly funded Salzburg buildings are already equipped with biomass heating plants in 2002. Here you can already see that policies can achieve very essential objectives. On and on, we also have sustainability as an agricultural policy goal. Minister of Agriculture and later Deputy Chancellor Joschi Riegler designed the eco-social agricultural policy in 1987. Minister Molterer was in his office at the time and has been responsible for this eco-social agricultural policy. If Austria already spends 40 percent of the total EU funding for environmental measures and another 20 percent for mining promotion, this shows that we are already spending 60 percent on this sustainability, while on the European average, only 20 percent are invested for this sustainability. In Agenda 2000, Austria again took a top place with the second pillar, rural development. 10 percent of all funds spent in the European Union for the second pillar benefit Austrian farmers alone. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! At national level, Austria has so far set considerable emphasis on sustainable development. An international study, the sustainability index, is giving a good witness to Austria. Austria – which Mr Böhacker has already mentioned – has reached the seventh place among the 142 countries under investigation. As regards quality of life, Austria has already taken first place. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! ==References== A strategy on sustainable development adopted in the Council of Ministers on April was drawn up, with a broad participation, by all departments, the countries, the municipalities, the social partners, in the context of the principle of long-term security of an ecological, economic and socially successful future formulated at the World Summit on Environment and Development in Rio de Janeiro in 1992. Our Federal Government has worked successfully for two years. In the interests of the Austrians, we wish her to continue to implement the reforms so successfully and not only for the next legislative period, but also for the Austrians.

Tax reductions are announced, tax increases and taxes are ultimately carried out: since the start of this government to a level of 111.5 billion schilling or in euro – as it doesn't sound so bad – 8,1 billion €. This makes 20 percent increase in the tax burden in just two years. However, in view of these data, the government no longer considers tax reform to be an ever-increasing burden. Ladies and gentlemen! A debate on a tax reform, as suggested by the Greens today, is certainly necessary, but such a debate, I believe, presupposes a debate on the distribution of the tax burden in Austria. If such a debate is to be held seriously, the following results will be achieved very quickly: The labour factor in Austria is much higher than the capital factor. This can be clearly demonstrated by many international comparisons. This means, ladies and gentlemen, that the existing tax system gives capital a competitive edge over work, and this competitive advantage is particularly dramatic in Austria. All considerations on a tax reform must therefore be considered, in our opinion, to be a priority to relieve labour. In essence, the Greens' request is also based on this. If this principle is accepted, ladies and gentlemen, then there is still a very crucial question. The question arises whether to reduce service providers' taxes, i.e. additional wage costs, or on the other hand, employees' taxes, i.e. wage taxes. At the latest, ladies and gentlemen, the spirits seem to separate – apparently also the red and the green. The only thing on which the government has so far been able to clearly determine itself is a reduction in additional labour costs, and the greens also seem to be primarily based on a reduction in additional labour costs. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! For the competitiveness of the economy, additional wage costs are not relevant, but total wage costs – that is, exactly the unit labour costs in relation to the trading partners that define the competitiveness of the Austrian economy. In the last five years, this cost position has developed in favour of Austria by almost 10 percentage points. I therefore see no reason for starting with a reduction in pay-related taxes on the share of the service providers – especially if the wage tax has increased significantly higher than those share of the service provider. Ladies and gentlemen! In addition to the cost reduction, the proportion of entrepreneurs in social security systems is reduced; in the other hand, it means that the proportion of employees is increasing or that benefits may be reduced – this bill is quite simple. We therefore believe that a reduction in additional labour costs is not the right way. We believe that the Austrian workers have now paid enough. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] It may not be that the cost reduction is so to speak, and, on the other hand, the workers have to put the rolls off the cake, so to speak. The SPÖ has therefore also chosen another way: We are concerned with strengthening mass purchasing power. To this end, we want to reduce wage and income tax, especially for small and medium-sized incomes, because this is exactly the group that has been most heavily taxed by the government. We are also concerned with creating jobs by, for example, proposing that the investment activities of companies be supported by an investment-free amount that is used to increase investment. Ladies and gentlemen! We see our proposal for a wage tax reduction as an emergency measure, so to speak, to counter the economic crisis and, on the other hand, to compensate for the decline that Austria had to take in the development of real income under this government. Of course, medium and long-term imbalances in the Austrian tax system must be effectively offset. That means, of course, such a long-term reform would also have to contain control elements as they have discussed the Greens today. In this sense, I am also quite on this line.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e17697	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! For the first time, we have had many years of criticism of the fact that there are ten different rights to award in Austria – a federal procurement right and nine country procurement rights – and that a lengthy administrative process is slowing down and this is not necessary. Ladies and gentlemen! I think the new draft has really been a success. But I would like to make a differentiation with the Greens: It does not automatically mean that all issues transferred to the countries for legislation must be resolved in this way. I am a supporter of the principle of subsidiarity, where, on a case-by-case basis, an examination must be carried out in the direction of whether a regulation would perhaps be more favourable at the country level or if a new system would be required at the federal level. In this case, I would be sure. Our accession to the European Union has also shown that we need to implement a European regulation and can now create a new federal procurement law for citizens and for legal users. In this case, yes – not generally. I would like to highlight a second point, because we should also make new regulations in the light of cost savings. I think it is very remarkable that this regulation is possible through the creation of the new Bundesbekauf GmbH savings, in the first phase of a percentage and in the second phase 2 percent of the purchase value in a longer-term perspective. Having already been pointed out today, what sizes this includes, I should like to remind you once again: In 1999, there were public procurements of 480 billion schillings. If one percent were calculated, this would mean a savings of 480 million schilling per year only by streamlining and reorganizing and the benefits of new media. These savings directly benefit the citizen because 480 million schilling can be invested more in the business and relieves the public. I think that this is an incredibly great saving that could be associated with such a new regulation. Third thought: Unlike Mrs Petrovic, I would like to point out that the Constitutional Court of First Instance then abolished part of this federal law because the control of awarding was unconstitutional, not the law itself. We have now provided for a provision that clearly regulates the establishment of a Federal Procurement Commission and a Federal Procurement Office for the control of the awarding bodies. That's constitutional. In this way, we also come to a temporary arrangement that, by the end of spring, this new provision can be put into effect and thus taken into account the recognition of the Constitutional Court. In conclusion, I note that this will allow for a modern legislation which saves enormous costs and which can rightly hope for a unanimous adoption in this House. Thank you.

In the past, the complexity of the procurement rules was that there were ten procurement rules, one at federal level and nine at country level. A summary of the rules of competence in the newly created Article 14b of the Federal Constitutional Act has made the German federal award a new form of competence in that legislation and enforcement are federal matters, while in the field of federal awarding legislation is the state case and the execution of state matters. Control in the field of the execution of national agendas is also carried out exclusively in the field of the country. Ladies and gentlemen! We know that the Constitutional Court has given this opportunity to abolish the law on grounds of equality, because there is a need for an efficient legal protection system in the so-called sub-suspension area – that is, in the tending rather lower calls for tenders. This was also introduced by the present Federal Procurement Act. I am also confident – I believe all the statements of the opposition leaders and the government parties as well – that there will be a uniform adoption of this law here. I am glad not only in the interest of the National Council, but especially in the interest of the countries and especially in the interest of those who have to apply this Federal Procurement Act in practice.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e17840	Mr President. High house! Ladies and gentlemen! This means taking into account environmental aspects in the performance description, mandatory compliance with ILO agreements, social implementation conditions, compliance with the provisions on barrier-free construction. A statement on the comments made by the Greens in the Committee, but also here today in plenary session: I would like to ask you, § 21 para. 6 and 7 to read with the mandatory provisions of the ILO Convention on the one hand and § 71 and § 80 Z. 14 on the other side. This takes into account what you have noted. Similarly, the requirements of the basic paper prepared by the countries have been taken into account, i.e. a systematic re-design of the material award schemes in the upper and lower thresholds, the creation of the necessary framework conditions for electronic procurement and e-business – i.e. a step into the future – and the creation of a new system of separate and non-disputable decisions in combination with pre-payment periods. Instead of ten award laws, there will now only be more of an award law as a basis for awarding public contracts. Legal protection remains with the countries and municipalities when awarding them. On the other hand, a Federal Award Office has been set up for the protection of the law at the time of the award of the Federal Republic of Germany with a slim administrative system as possible. This has been pointed out several times and has also been valued. The Federal Procurement Control Commission has been restructured into a mediation body. In this sense, as I believe, we can be satisfied with the work that lies behind us, and we can also face each other, which means that when different interests are dealt with in such a conflict-free field by this House and the offices of the communities and countries. – Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e18155	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! It has been discussed several times today about the dimension of this law. It is important that there is also a functioning competition in the award of deliveries and services, of course also in the interests of the economy, that access to competition is guaranteed for all and that fair competition is also achieved in the sense and in the interests of the economy. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] In my opinion, both will be fulfilled by this law. Many discussions have also succeeded in taking both interests into account and, of course, as has already been said, in complying with the EU directive on this matter. The quantum leap of this law is undoubtedly the fact that it will be uniform, as all previous speakers have already said. This was of course also the reason for the fact that the birth of this law lasted a little longer, because the countries have not so easily renounced their previous competence. The people suffering from this situation were, of course, the economy and the commercials. As one who is very often involved in the award of a job, I know what I am talking about here. No one in the past could understand why, from a national border, a provider has suddenly to take into account other legal provisions in the award of services, and that in a unified European Economic Area. It is therefore a historic merit of this federal government that it has succeeded in reaching the agreement of the countries for this shift of competence that there are no longer ten different laws, but only one more. This, of course, brings – and that must be said – administrative simplification and economic kindness. The legislation will be federal. The execution will be a federal matter where the award of the federal government is concerned, and where the issue of awarding in the field of the countries and communities is concerned, the execution will be national. The control will be the same, and legal protection will also be divided into federal and country, depending on whether it is federal or municipal award. One thing is also essential: In the future, the legal control of federal contracts is a completely newly structured Federal Procurement Office. In the future, a tripartite will decide. This too is an improvement that can be seen. It is also encouraging and important that there will be improvements in some areas for small and medium-sized enterprises. As an example of this, I mention the principle of subcontracting, which was explained in the law, according to which contracts should be awarded in principle by subject areas, with the decision – which is clear – to be taken by the client. For example, the Lower Austrian Government has already decided that the mandate of general companies should only take place if there are important reasons for this. Otherwise, it should be allocated by subject area. This is particularly important and in the interest of smaller companies, because you know that it has become more and more unsitting to have been awarded in too large tranches or by general contractors and the smaller businesses had no chance at all to come to orders. The second improvement in the interest of small and medium-sized enterprises concerns the clarification in the law that speculative and underpriced offers are to be excluded in the future. This is valued in § 98 Z 3 of the Law. I also consider this to be positive: that speculators and black sheep in the industry will not have a chance to come to an order in the future. Finally, positive is the agreement reached after the Committee meeting that the threshold for mental-creative services is increased to € 130 000 in the lower threshold in the non-open procedure. This is positive in that it is generally difficult to describe as a spiritual-creative achievement and because there is usually only a small number of interested parties, or at all only one interested person, and therefore an open procedure costs unnecessary money and time. Finally, I would like to mention the electronic auction. The electronic auction up to a threshold of €40 000 is something new in the law, is something that corresponds to the trend and is already being practiced in some countries. On the other hand, there were concerns about smaller businesses, because there is, of course, a risk that this electronic auction will be a price dumping, but it cannot be concluded that this electronic auction has been incorporated into the law. One will have to see if it does not really come at a price dumping. Finally, the anchoring of the contractor disaster is also positive in the law, because this will also lead to simplification and red tape savings. So all in all: a positive law that we like to agree with.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e19595	Mr President. High house! The Court of Auditors is under constitutional obligation to submit every two years a report containing the data on the average income and pensions of the public economy. He presented this report on the years 1999 and 2000. The report has been discussed in great detail in the Committee and, as I see it now in plenary, has found great support. There were a number of words about it. But I would like to stress: Not for nothing does this report mean: Report on the data on average income and public-sector pensions. It is therefore necessary to record the income and pension data of the Court of Auditors, to check for plausibility and then to report to the House. I emphasize: Income and pension data. Although it has rightly been said here – and the Court of Auditors itself has noted this in its report – that we can only check this data for its plausibility, I think that the finding is justified that whenever individual data has been subject to a closer review, even to the suggestion and question of Members – that is for the interest of Members of this House – these data have still proved to be true. But these are data on income and pensions. It is not employment-related data. I would like to maintain this with all clarity, because, regardless of the length of this report and the ability to express it, we must not want to read too much from the report, nor should we want to put too much into this report. That is, if one wants a report on employment policy relevant data, then this report would have to look different and then the data collection would have to be different. I am very aware of this with regard to the discussion already held in the Committee and now in plenary, whether the number of apprentices in the public sector has fallen or increased. I would just like to say very carefully: We know that in order to reorganise the Bundestheaterverband, to this extent, changes have been made, which we also noted in the report; but these restructurings in the Bundestheaterverband in principle resulted in a different situation in 2000 compared to that in 1999. I do not wish to be the highest representative of the Court of Auditors. I would like to express this clearly in order not to give a false impression, namely that this report gives even more than it already does. I would also like to warn against creating longer periods of time and drawing certain conclusions from them. It was in the Committee's opinion that time series should be drawn up over ten years, and then the relevant analyses should be carried out. I would also like to warn against that. I would like to remind you: We do not have the same companies as we did ten years ago, we have much less! Five years ago, the Court of Auditors had to include around 500 and more enterprises in this income survey. Now it's not even 300 anymore. Therefore: I think it is extremely dangerous to draw up time series under these conditions and to carry out final analyses of employment policy. I am therefore going to distance myself from this because, in my opinion, this can only result in a distorted picture in toto, that is, in total for all public undertakings. If there is an interest in it, then you should use the data relating to the individual company. In this respect, it is quite possible to make reliable statements about a longer period of time, but also this with certain reservations, because there were restructurings there, because there were some missing competences, partly because mergers took place and more so. I should therefore like to warn against putting more into this report, but also more to read out than is in it. High house! Some Members have referred to the fact that this report does not contain an actual audit report from the Court of Auditors. I can fully confirm this and have already expressed it several times in the past. This report is a statistical survey. What has been suggested by some Members of Parliament, namely that it might be more favourable to put these statistical surveys into other hands, in hands that work every day with statistics that have to operate with statistics that collect statistical data, I can only fully emphasize. I would also like to say that I do not regard statistical work as less than that of the Court of Auditors, but it is a different activity. Everyone should actually do the best they can, and these are the auditors in the Court of Auditors and, for example, the statistics in the "Statistics Austria". If, in this House, a process of thinking has been initiated as to whether or not to relocate the competences for drawing up this report, I can assure you: You have the Court of Auditors on your side in this regard. I am pleased that this report has been adopted and that thanks have been given to the Court of Auditors, but I should like to ask that this consideration continue in this House, perhaps in order to achieve a result that will satisfy both the Court of Auditors and this House and perhaps also the "Statistics Austria". - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e20036	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! Very soon: At the plenary session, held on 18 February. The tax-law reform law was dealt with in April 2002. It is a somewhat complicated title, but it at least gives at least the contents of the law. This matter of law was discussed in detail at the time, but in the end no legislative decision was taken. At the heart of this law is the creation of an independent financial senate, as we all know, and I have criticised, in the context of this then debate on behalf of my group, that a matter relating to the administrative reform law and consequently to tax money, namely the extension of the flexibilisation clause, is not on the agenda and thus the decision-making process. Today, she is doing well, and this also shows that we have been on the right track with the procedure we have proposed. In the context of the discussions in the Financial Committee, representatives of our group have already explained why we see an objective link between these two materials and are only willing to ensure the required two-thirds majority in the two legislative documents if our ideas on this matter, which were clearly objective, are taken into account to a large extent. During the debate, representatives of the two government parties tried to bring me up with a flexibilization clause. We said at the time: We are prepared to agree under certain conditions, we are once again talking about it. In principle, I have put forward our agreement on both of the materials. We were just not to have done five before twelve two so important materials, for which a two-thirds majority is required. Their reaction was rather unusual, ladies and gentlemen. You have made it possible to negotiate again through your behaviour. In these parliamentary talks – which were certainly very hard discussions – we were able to achieve an extension of the flexibilization clause, and thus the conditions for our consent to the tax-law reform law were created. I say once again what I have emphasized several times, ladies and gentlemen: We are ready to guarantee and go with the two-thirds majority in the case of essential materials, but we want to have detailed discussions, we are not for Husch-Fusch procedures, where there is no time to explain their ideas, and we only insist that it be done. I would like to stress once again: As with previous important legislative instruments, we are willing to give our consent within the framework of the representative. I would just like to point out one thing – and I ask you not to be misunderstood: We are the strongest group in this House and we have a commitment to our voters. If we assume responsibility, we do not agree easily, but only under certain conditions. Even in the debate on financial market supervision, a hugely important matter, we have tried to carry out our ideas in the first run-up. They negated it at the time; in the second run it worked thank God. Today, as I have put it on behalf of my group, we are going to vote in favour of both this tax-applicable-reform law and the extension of the flexibilisation clause. Allow me, in three sentences, to say what this law is all about and why it is so important in our view. With the independent financial Senate, we are convinced that the legal protection standard will be increased. There is no doubt that the independent financial Senate will bring a harmonization in the case law, because, although dislodged, there is a single authority. Finally, we hope that – Mr Böhacker has already said this in the last debate – it will lead to a reduction in the number of cases before the Administrative Court. However, it is not, ladies and gentlemen, to say yes automatically to everything. In the discussion on this issue, the organization in financial management was discussed – I say it carefully – and it says the closing of financial offices in the area. You will not get any consent from us, ladies and gentlemen, if you really want to. I would like to understand that our fellow Members, especially those from the regions, attach great importance to the fact that a separate vote on this point, on Article VII(1), is taking place. My colleague, Mr Hagenhofer, will justify this and put forward a motion for a resolution, and my colleagues will continue to deal with this issue as a whole. – Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-AT_2002-05-22-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00103_d7e20119	Mr President. Mr Finanzminister! My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! The legislative papers that we now have for decision-making are an example of good cooperation in the Committee – something that unfortunately is very rarely mentioned in the media reports. Since Mr Edlinger is signaling me through his mimik what he has told me today, I would like to start my speech with it. As has been said, this package of laws is a good example of the culture of discussion and negotiation that we have in the Financial Committee. This is certainly the merit of all the committee members, especially those of my predecessor, Mr Kurt Heindl, who is the chairman of the Financial Committee. I would also like to say, however, that Mr Edlinger, who often plays his opposition role on the speaker's desk here, and who is verbally active in verbal attacks, is prepared to cooperate in the committee in an objective and constructive manner. I would like to expressly acknowledge this culture of dialogue and negotiation, which exists in the Financial Committee. During the last plenary session, we have already discussed the major reform project "independent financial senates" in terms of content. I myself, with a colleague from the FPÖ, with Hermann Böhacker, then made the request to postpone the third reading, because we were unable to reach agreement on the points of the Constitution. We said at the time that we were not willing to allow a junkimization of this important matter with other materials, but of course we agreed to find a solution for another subject, namely the famous flexibilization clause in federal budgetary law. We then tried – and the attempt was fortunately successful – to solve both problems. I am really happy today that we did not vote at third reading last time, have postponed this vote, and have thus received the opportunity to adopt both legislative instruments today. I do not want to say anything on the content of the independent financial statements, since we have already held this discussion here in plenary for the last time. I just want to say so much: After the supervision of the financial market, we have achieved another major reform project, which we are glad to have received the constitutional majority with the help of the opposition parties because of the good negotiating and discussion climate in the financial committee. The other point, namely the extension of the flexibilisation clause, has really moved all the political groups. The starting point of the SPÖ was that it should be extended as it now stands in the law. On the other hand, we have said: The flexibilization clause is a good instrument, it is praised by everyone, but actually it is strange that it is applied by so few. There's something wrong with that! Our consideration was: It appears that the motivation for the budgetary bodies, for the specialist departments, to introduce this flexibilisation clause into their departments is not sufficient. I believe that the solution we have now found is a good condition for the future use of more specialist departments and more organisational units than has been the case so far. Our consideration was as follows: If a facility is good, then it cannot be that only in three resorts, seven organizational units make use of it. I very much hope that the construction we have now found, namely the creation of an agreement between the Minister of Finance and the Minister of Finance on the use of surpluses - from positive balances of conduct, that is, I believe, in the law - can improve the situation so far. It is therefore to be agreed between the Minister of Finance and the Minister of Finance, which proportion of the respective organisational unit is given and how the remainder is to be divided. In addition, we have also taken up the suggestion of Mr Edlinger to make a new deadline, namely until 2006. This very nice example confirms once again my two-world theory: I experience the working world on the one hand, and there we have very constructive discussions, and on the other hand I also experience the media world, where the mutual attacks naturally form the headlines. This is an appearance which unfortunately does not reflect the constructive cooperation we have in the Financial Committee. Finally, I should like to say to all the political groups that I am very happy that we have such a good negotiating climate in the financial committee. What I can contribute to this will continue to exist in the future, I will be happy to do so.
